I don’t want to give you a wrong answer or make it sound worse than it is. I guess I can’t really think of a clear time right now. Yeah… I think that does happen sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound like I never pay attention, because I can start off really trying.

Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble staying focused or organized in a setting that wasn’t related to schoolwork or your accessories shop—like hanging out with friends, doing something you enjoy, or even just trying to relax at home?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m honestly not sure. I’m trying to think of an example that isn’t school or the shop, but that’s mostly where I notice it because it’s the most obvious and stressful.\n\nLike at home or with friends, I don’t know if I’d call it the same thing… maybe I just don’t pay attention to it as much. I’m not sure if that makes sense, but it’s been getting pretty overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried a lot of the time, even when there isn't a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like all the time for no reason. I mean, I do get worried when I’m behind on things or if I feel like I messed something up, but I feel like that’s kind of normal, right? I wouldn’t say I’m just anxious out of nowhere most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when things aren’t particularly stressful?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I have bad moods sometimes, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m just sad for no reason or not interested in things I like. I still care about stuff. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it really hard to get started on tasks, even ones you know you need to do or usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I just refuse to do things, because I do know they need to get done. It’s more like with schoolwork and my little accessories shop, everything gets mixed together—supplies, due dates, receipts, assignment notes—and then I’ll sit there looking at it like, okay, where do I even start? And then it kind of turns into piles, and I feel embarrassed because it probably looks really messy. Is that what you mean by getting started?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities, even when you know what needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I know what needs to get done, but when it’s schoolwork and my little accessories shop at the same time, it gets really messy. Like, supplies, due dates, receipts, assignment notes—everything ends up in piles, and then I’m just staring at it trying to figure out what comes first.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m not trying or anything. I just… I don’t seem to have a good system, and then it gets overwhelming. Does that count as trouble organizing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay organized or keep your thoughts in order even in situations that aren’t related to schoolwork or your accessories shop—like keeping your room tidy, following through on plans with friends, or remembering to do everyday things?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I notice it most with school and the shop, honestly. Like that’s the reason I came in, because I feel like I can’t keep those organized at the same time. My supplies and due dates and receipts and assignment notes all end up in piles, and then I don’t know what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nFor everyday stuff, I’m not sure if it’s as bad… or maybe I just don’t pay attention to it the same way. But the main thing is definitely that everything gets mixed together and I can’t seem to keep it in order. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble staying focused or organized in a setting that wasn’t related to schoolwork or your accessories shop—like hanging out with friends, doing something you enjoy, or even just trying to relax at home?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m honestly not sure. I’m trying to think of an example that isn’t school or the shop, but that’s mostly where I notice it because it’s the most obvious and stressful.\n\nLike at home or with friends, I don’t know if I’d call it the same thing… maybe I just don’t pay attention to it as much. I don’t want to give you a wrong answer or make it sound worse than it is. I guess I can’t really think of a clear time right now. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy even when things weren’t particularly stressful or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not really in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, I have days where I feel tired or kind of blah, but usually it feels tied to being stressed or having too much going on. I don’t think I’ve just randomly lost interest in things I like when everything was otherwise okay. I don’t want to make it sound like I never pay attention, because I can start off really trying.\n\nLike in class, or if I’m watching a video for my business assignments, I’ll be listening at first, and then after a few minutes I realize my brain has gone off to, like, charm designs or ideas for orders. Is that what you mean by getting started?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities, even when you know what needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I know what needs to get done, but when it’s schoolwork and my little accessories shop at the same time, it gets really messy. Like, supplies, due dates, receipts, assignment notes—everything ends up in piles, and then I’m just staring at it trying to figure out what comes first.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m not trying or anything. I just… I don’t seem to have a good system, and then it gets overwhelming. Does that count as trouble organizing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay organized or keep your thoughts in order even in situations that aren’t related to schoolwork or your accessories shop—like keeping your room tidy, following through on plans with friends, or remembering to do everyday things?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I notice it most with school and the shop, honestly. Like that’s the reason I came in, because I feel like I can’t keep those organized at the same time. My supplies and due dates and receipts and assignment notes all end up in piles, and then I don’t know what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nFor everyday stuff, I’m not sure if it’s as bad… or maybe I just don’t pay attention to it the same way. But the main thing is definitely that everything gets mixed together and I can’t seem to keep it in order. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble staying focused or organized in a setting that wasn’t related to schoolwork or your accessories shop—like hanging out with friends, doing something you enjoy, or even just trying to relax at home?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m honestly not sure. I’m trying to think of an example that isn’t school or the shop, but that’s mostly where I notice it because it’s the most obvious and stressful.\n\nLike at home or with friends, I don’t know if I’d call it the same thing… maybe I just don’t pay attention to it as much. I don’t want to give you a wrong answer or make it sound worse than it is. I guess I can’t really think of a clear time right now. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy even when things weren’t particularly stressful or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not really in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, I have days where I feel tired or kind of blah, but usually it feels tied to being stressed or having too much going on. I don’t think I’ve just randomly lost interest in things I like when everything was otherwise okay. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when things weren’t particularly stressful and you still felt down, sad, or didn’t enjoy things as much as usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can think of a clear time like that, no. I might be forgetting something, but nothing specific comes to mind. Is that an okay answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks or activities once you've started them, even if they're things you care about or enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound like I never pay attention, because I can start off really trying.\n\nLike in class, or if I’m watching a video for my business assignments, I’ll be listening at first, and then after a few minutes I realize my brain has gone off to, like, charm designs or ideas for orders. I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble staying focused or organized while doing something completely unrelated to school or your accessories shop—like following a conversation, planning something with friends, or even just reading something for fun?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe the closest example is when I’m watching videos for my business assignments, or in class. Then I’m like, wait, I missed half of what they just said.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to ignore it or anything. My mind just kind of goes somewhere else. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of a time when you felt down, hopeless, or stopped enjoying things for at least two weeks, even when there wasn’t anything particularly stressful happening in your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Um… I don’t think so. Not for like two weeks straight, especially if nothing stressful was going on.
